**Philadelphia** ([/ˌfɪləˈdɛlfiə/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/1/1d/LL-Q1860\_%28eng%29-Flame%2C_not_lame-Philadelphia.wav/LL-Q1860\_%28eng%29-Flame%2C_not_lame-Philadelphia.wav.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/1/1d/LL-Q1860_%28eng%29-Flame%2C_not_lame-Philadelphia.wav/LL-Q1860_%28eng%29-Flame%2C_not_lame-Philadelphia.wav.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup> [*FIL-ə-DEL-fee-ə*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Pronunciation_respelling_key)), colloquially referred to as **Philly**, is the [most populous city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_municipalities_in_Pennsylvania) in the U.S. state of [Pennsylvania](23332.md) and the [sixth-most populous city in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_cities_by_population).[^cite_note-United_States_Census_Bureau-2-11] Its population was 1.60 million at the 2020 census and estimated at 1.57 million in 2025.[^cite_note-PhiladelphiaQuickFacts-6] The [Philadelphia metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_metropolitan_area), also known as the Delaware Valley, is located in both the [Mid-Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mid-Atlantic_United_States) and [northeastern United States](431669.md); home to 6.33 million residents, it is the nation's [ninth-largest metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_statistical_area).[^cite_note-CensusState2020-8] Philadelphia is known for [its culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Philadelphia), [cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_cuisine), and [history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Philadelphia), maintaining contemporary influence in [technology and business](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Philadelphia), sports, and music.[^cite_note-12][^cite_note-13]

Philadelphia was founded in 1682 by [William Penn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Penn), an English [Quaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quaker) and advocate of [religious freedom](46006.md), and served as the capital of the colonial era [Province of Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Pennsylvania).[^cite_note-W\._W\._Norton_&_Company-1982-3]<sup>: 4–5 </sup>[^cite_note-InsightGuides2005-14]<sup>: 21–22 </sup> It then played a vital role during the [American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution) and [Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War). It served as the central meeting place for the [nation's Founding Fathers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States) in hosting the [First Continental Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Continental_Congress) (1774) and the [Second Continental Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Continental_Congress), during which the Founders formed the [Continental Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Army), elected [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington) as its commander, and adopted the [Declaration of Independence](31874.md) on July 4, 1776. During the Revolutionary War's [Philadelphia campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_campaign), the city fell to the [British Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Army_during_the_American_Revolutionary_War), which occupied Philadelphia for nine months from September 1777 to June 1778.[^cite_note-Ellis-2007-15] Following the end of the Revolutionary War, the [U.S. Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Constitution) was ratified at the [Philadelphia Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Convention). Philadelphia remained the largest U.S. city until 1790, and it served as the nation's first capital from May 10, 1775, until December 12, 1776, and on [four subsequent occasions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_capitals_in_the_United_States) until 1800, when construction of the new national capital in [Washington, D.C.](108956.md) was completed.[^cite_note-16]

With [17 four-year universities and colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_colleges_and_universities_in_Philadelphia) in the city, Philadelphia is one of the nation's leading centers for higher education and [academic research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_research).[^cite_note-17][^cite_note-Sisak-2015-18] As of 2023, the Philadelphia metropolitan area had a [gross metropolitan product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._metropolitan_areas_by_GDP) of US\$557.6 billion[^cite_note-fred.stlouisfed.org-19] and is home to 13 [*Fortune* 500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortune_500) corporate headquarters.[^cite_note-20] Metropolitan Philadelphia ranks as one of the nation's Big Five [tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_technology) [venture capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venture_capital) hubs, facilitated by its proximity central to both the [financial ecosystems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wall_Street) of [New York City](645042.md) and the regulatory environment of Washington, D.C.[^cite_note-Maloney-2024-21] Metropolitan Philadelphia is also a [biotechnology](4502.md) hub and has garnered the nickname "Cellicon Valley" for its central role in the development of [immunotherapies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immunotherapies) to treat different cancers.[^cite_note-22] The [Philadelphia Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Stock_Exchange), owned by [Nasdaq](21559.md) since 2008, is the nation's oldest stock exchange and a global leader in [options](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Option_\(finance\)) trading.[^cite_note-Lucas_Downey_and_Somer_Anderson-2022-23] [30th Street Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/30th_Street_Station), an important national railway crossroads, is the [third-busiest Amtrak rail hub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_busiest_Amtrak_stations) in the U.S., handling over 5.5 million passengers in 2025. In part aided by its central Mid-Atlantic U.S. geolocation, [Philadelphia International Airport](262559.md) has become the largest [transatlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatlantic_crossing) hub for [American Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Airlines).[^cite_note-24] The city's [multimodal transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multimodal_transportation) and logistics infrastructure also includes the expanding [PhilaPort seaport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Philadelphia),[^cite_note-25] and [Interstate 95](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_95_in_Pennsylvania), the spine of the north–south highway axis linking the cities of the U.S. [Eastern Seaboard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Seaboard).

Philadelphia has a culturally and philanthropically active [LGBTQ+ community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_culture_in_Philadelphia). The city has also played an [influential historic and ongoing role](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Philadelphia) in the development and evolution of American music, especially [R\&B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R\&B), [soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul_music), and rock.[^cite_note-26][^cite_note-27] The city hosts more outdoor sculptures and murals than any other city in the nation.[^cite_note-28][^cite_note-29] [Fairmount Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairmount_Park), when combined with adjacent [Wissahickon Valley Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wissahickon_Valley_Park) in the same [watershed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drainage_basin), has an area of 2,052 acres (830 ha), representing one of the nation's largest urban parks and the [world's 55th largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_urban_parks_by_size).[^cite_note-tpl.org-30] With [five professional sports teams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_in_Philadelphia) and one of the nation's most loyal and passionate fan bases, Philadelphia is often ranked as the nation's best city for professional sports fans.[^cite_note-31][^cite_note-32][^cite_note-33][^cite_note-34]

Philadelphia is a city of many firsts, including the nation's first [library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Library_Company_of_Philadelphia) (1731),[^cite_note-USHistory-35] [hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Hospital) (1751),[^cite_note-USHistory-35] [medical school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perelman_School_of_Medicine_at_the_University_of_Pennsylvania) (1765),[^cite_note-36] [national capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Continental_Congress) (1774),[^cite_note-37] [university (by some accounts)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_university_in_the_United_States) (1779),[^cite_note-38] [central bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_North_America) (1781),[^cite_note-39] [stock exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Stock_Exchange) (1790),[^cite_note-USHistory-35] [zoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Zoo) (1874),[^cite_note-PhilaZoo-40] and [business school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wharton_School_of_the_University_of_Pennsylvania) (1881).[^cite_note-41] Philadelphia hosts 67 [National Historic Landmarks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_National_Historic_Landmarks_in_Philadelphia), including [Independence Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Hall).[^cite_note-42][^cite_note-43][^cite_note-Sisak-2015-18] From the city's 17th century founding through the present, Philadelphia has been the birthplace of or home to many [prominent and influential Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_people_from_Philadelphia).

## History

Main article: [History of Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Philadelphia)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Philadelphia).

See also: [List of National Historic Landmarks in Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_National_Historic_Landmarks_in_Philadelphia) and [National Register of Historic Places listings in Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Register_of_Historic_Places_listings_in_Philadelphia)

### Native peoples

Before the arrival of Europeans in the early 17th century, the [Lenape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lenape), an [Indian tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States) also known as the Delaware Indians, lived in the village of [Shackamaxon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shackamaxon) in present-day Philadelphia and the surrounding area.[^cite_note-Josephy_188-44] The Lenape historically lived along the [Delaware River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_River) [watershed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drainage_basin), western [Long Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_Island), and the [Lower Hudson Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Hudson_Valley).[^cite_note-45] Most Lenape were pushed out of the region during the 18th century as the original [Thirteen Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteen_Colonies) expanded, which was further exacerbated by losses from intertribal conflicts.[^cite_note-Josephy_188-44] Lenape communities were also weakened by newly introduced diseases, mainly [smallpox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smallpox), and conflicts with Europeans. The [Iroquois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iroquois) occasionally fought the Lenape. Surviving Lenape moved west into the upper [Ohio River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_River) basin. Following the [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War) and the subsequent establishment of the United States, the Lenape began moving further west. In the 1860s, the [U.S. federal government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._federal_government) sent most remaining Lenape in the [eastern United States](89126.md) to the [Indian Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Territory) in present-day [Oklahoma](22489.md) and surrounding territories as part of the [Indian removal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_removal) policy.

### Colonial era



[William Penn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Penn) (holding paper) and [King Charles II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_II_of_England) depicted in *The Birth of Pennsylvania 1680*, a portrait by [Jean Leon Gerome Ferris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Leon_Gerome_Ferris)



William Penn's 1682 [Treaty of Shackamaxon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Shackamaxon) with the [Lenape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lenape) tribe depicted in *[Penn's Treaty with the Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penn's_Treaty_with_the_Indians)*, a 1772 portrait by [Benjamin West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_West)

A Portraiture of the City of Philadelphia, by Thomas Holme

A 1683 portrait of Philadelphia by [Thomas Holme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Holme), believed to be the city's first map, bounded by the [Delaware River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_River) to the east and the [Schuylkill River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schuylkill_River) to the west.

Europeans first entered Philadelphia and the surrounding [Delaware Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_Valley) in the early 17th century. The first settlements were founded by [Dutch colonists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_colonization_of_the_Americas), who built [Fort Nassau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Nassau_\(South_River\)) on the [Delaware River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_River) in 1623 in what is now [Brooklawn, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brooklawn,_New_Jersey). The Dutch considered the entire Delaware River valley to be part of their [New Netherland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Netherland) colony. In 1638, Swedish settlers led by renegade Dutch established the colony of [New Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Sweden) at [Fort Christina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Christina), located in present-day [Wilmington, Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilmington,_Delaware), and quickly spread out in the valley. In 1644, New Sweden supported the [Susquehannocks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susquehannock) in their war against [Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Maryland) colonists.[^cite_note-46] In 1648, the Dutch built [Fort Beversreede](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Beversreede) on the west bank of the Delaware, south of the [Schuylkill River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schuylkill_River) near the present-day [Eastwick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastwick,_Philadelphia) section of Philadelphia, to reassert their dominion over the area. The [Swedes](29380512.md) responded by building [Fort Nya Korsholm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Sweden#Forts), or New [Korsholm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korsholm), named after a town in Finland with a Swedish majority.

In 1655, a [Dutch military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_military) campaign led by New Netherland Director-General [Peter Stuyvesant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Stuyvesant) took control of the Swedish colony, ending its claim to independence. The Swedish and [Finnish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_people) settlers continued to have their own [militia](20622.md), religion, and court, and to enjoy substantial autonomy under the Dutch. An English fleet captured the New Netherland colony in 1664, though the situation did not change substantially until 1682, when the area was included in [William Penn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Penn)'s charter for Pennsylvania.[^cite_note-InsightGuides2005-14]<sup>: 21–22 </sup>

In 1681, in partial repayment of a debt, [Charles II of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_II_of_England) granted Penn a [charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charter) for what would become the [Pennsylvania colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Pennsylvania). Despite the royal charter, Penn bought the land from the local [Lenape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lenape) in an effort to establish good terms with the Native Americans and ensure peace for the colony.[^cite_note-InsightGuides2005-14]<sup>: 21 </sup> Penn made a [treaty of friendship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Shackamaxon) with Lenape chief [Tammany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tammany) under an elm tree at [Shackamaxon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shackamaxon), in what is now the city's [Fishtown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fishtown,_Philadelphia) neighborhood.[^cite_note-W\._W\._Norton_&_Company-1982-3]<sup>: 4–5 </sup> Penn named the city Philadelphia, which is Greek for 'brotherly love', derived from the [Ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek) terms [φίλος](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%CF%86%CE%AF%CE%BB%CE%BF%CF%82) *phílos* ('beloved, dear') and [ἀδελφός](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E1%BC%80%CE%B4%CE%B5%CE%BB%CF%86%CF%8C%CF%82) *adelphós* ('brother, brotherly'). There were a number of cities named *[Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_\(disambiguation\)#Ancient)* ([Φιλαδέλφεια](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%CE%A6%CE%B9%CE%BB%CE%B1%CE%B4%CE%AD%CE%BB%CF%86%CE%B5%CE%B9%CE%B1)) in the [Eastern Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Mediterranean) during the Greek and Roman periods, including modern [Alaşehir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ala%C5%9Fehir), mentioned as the site of an early Christian congregation in the [Book of Revelation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Revelation). As a [Quaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quaker), Penn had experienced [religious persecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_persecution) and wanted his colony to be a place where anyone could worship freely. This tolerance, which exceeded that of other colonies, led to better relations with the local native tribes and fostered Philadelphia's rapid growth into America's most important city.[^cite_note-47]

Penn planned a city on the Delaware River to serve as a port and place for government. Hoping that Philadelphia would become more like an English rural town instead of a city, Penn laid out roads on a [grid plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grid_plan) to keep houses and businesses spread far apart with areas for gardens and [orchards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orchard).

The city's inhabitants did not follow Penn's plans, however, and instead crowded the present-day [Port of Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Philadelphia) on the Delaware River and subdivided and resold their lots.[^cite_note-W\._W\._Norton_&_Company-1982-3]<sup>: 7, 14–16 </sup> Before Penn left Philadelphia for the final time, he issued the Charter of 1701 establishing it as a city. Though poor at first, Philadelphia became an important trading center with tolerable living conditions by the 1750s. [Benjamin Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Franklin), a leading citizen, helped improve city services and founded new ones that were among the first in the nation, including a [fire company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Fire_Company), [library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Library_Company_of_Philadelphia), and [hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Hospital).

A number of [philosophical societies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy) were formed, which were centers of the city's intellectual life, including the Philadelphia Society for Promoting Agriculture (1785), the Pennsylvania Society for the Encouragement of Manufactures and the Useful Arts (1787), the [Academy of Natural Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_of_Natural_Sciences) (1812), and the [Franklin Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_Institute) (1824).[^cite_note-48] These societies developed and financed new industries that attracted skilled and knowledgeable immigrants from Europe.

### American Revolution

See also: [Pennsylvania in the American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_in_the_American_Revolution) and [Philadelphia campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_campaign)



The [Committee of Five](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_of_Five) presenting their draft of the [Declaration of Independence](31874.md) in [Independence Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Hall) on June 28, 1776, depicted in [an 1818 painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_Independence_\(painting\)) by [John Trumbull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Trumbull); historian [Joseph Ellis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Ellis) called the Declaration "the most potent and consequential words in American history."

[^cite_note-49]



[President's House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President's_House_\(Philadelphia\)) on [Market Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_Street_\(Philadelphia\)) served as the presidential mansion for the nation's first two presidents, [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington) and [John Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Adams), from 1790 to 1800 before the completion of the [White House](33057.md) and the development of Washington, D.C. as the nation's new capital.



[Independence Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Hall) on [Chestnut Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chestnut_Street_\(Philadelphia\)) between 5th and 6th streets, where the [Declaration of Independence](31874.md) was signed and the [Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Constitution) was ratified, on July 4, 1776, and June 21, 1788, respectively

Philadelphia's importance and central location in the colonies made it a natural center for [America's revolutionaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution). By the 1750s, Philadelphia surpassed [Boston](24437894.md) as the largest city and busiest port in [British America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_America), and the second-largest city in the entire [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) after London.[^cite_note-50][^cite_note-51] In 1774, as resentment of the [British government's](25318118.md) policies towards the colonies and support for independence began burgeoning in the colonies, Philadelphia hosted the [First Continental Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Continental_Congress) at [Carpenters' Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpenters'_Hall), and 12 of the original 13 colonies sent delegates to the Congress.

From 1775 to 1781, Philadelphia hosted the [Second Continental Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Continental_Congress),[^cite_note-52] whose 56 delegates unanimously adopted the [Declaration of Independence](31874.md) inside what was then called Pennsylvania State House and was later renamed [Independence Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Hall). Written predominantly by [Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson) from his second-floor apartment on [Market Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_Street_\(Philadelphia\)) within walking distance of Independence Hall, the Declaration has been described by [Pulitzer Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulitzer_Prize_for_History)-winning historian [Joseph Ellis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Ellis) as "the most potent and consequential words in American history,"[^cite_note-Ellis-2007-15] and its adoption represented a declaration of war against [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain). Since the Declaration's July 4, 1776, adoption, its signing has been cited globally and repeatedly by various peoples of the world seeking independence and liberty. It also has been, since its adoption, the basis for annual celebration by Americans; in 1938, this celebration of the Declaration was formalized as [Independence Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_\(United_States\)), one of only [eleven designated U.S. federal holidays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_holidays_in_the_United_States).

After [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington)'s defeat at the [Battle of Brandywine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Brandywine) in [Chadds Ford Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chadds_Ford_Township), on September 11, 1777, during the [Philadelphia campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_campaign), the revolutionary capital of Philadelphia was defenseless, and the city prepared for what was perceived to be an inevitable British attack. Because bells could easily be recast into munitions, the [Liberty Bell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty_Bell), then known as the Pennsylvania State Bell, and bells from two Philadelphia churches, [Christ Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_Church,_Philadelphia) and [St. Peter's Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Peter's_Episcopal_Church_\(Philadelphia\)), were hastily taken down and transported by heavily guarded wagon train out of the city. The Liberty Bell was taken to [Zion German Reformed Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_German_Evangelical_Reformed_Church) in Northampton Town, which is present-day [Allentown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allentown,_Pennsylvania), where it was hidden under the church's floor boards for nine months from September 1777 until departure of British forces from Philadelphia in June 1778.[^cite_note-53] Two Revolutionary War battles, the [Siege of Fort Mifflin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Fort_Mifflin), fought between September 26 and November 16, 1777, and the [Battle of Germantown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Germantown), fought on October 4, 1777, took place within Philadelphia's city limits.

In Philadelphia, the Second Continental Congress adopted the [Articles of Confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Articles_of_Confederation) on November 15, 1777. Independence Hall in Philadelphia was the meeting place for the [Constitutional Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Convention_\(United_States\)), which ratified the [Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Constitution) on September 17, 1787, which is now the longest-standing codified national constitution.

Philadelphia served as capital of the United States on [five separate occasions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_capitals_in_the_United_States) between 1776 and 1800—in sum, most of the colonial and early post-colonial period, including the decade before 1800, when Washington, D.C., became the national capital.[^cite_note-54] In 1793, the largest [yellow fever epidemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1793_Philadelphia_yellow_fever_epidemic) in U.S. history killed about 4,000 to 5,000 people in Philadelphia, or about ten percent of the city's population at the time.[^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56]

The state capital was moved from Philadelphia to [Lancaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancaster,_Pennsylvania) in 1799, then ultimately to [Harrisburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harrisburg) in 1812. Philadelphia remained the nation's largest city until the late 18th century. It also was the nation's financial and cultural center until ultimately being eclipsed in total population by [New York City](645042.md) in 1790. In 1816, the city's free Black community founded the [African Methodist Episcopal Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Methodist_Episcopal_Church), the first independent Black denomination in the country, and the first Black [Episcopal Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_Church_\(United_States\)). The free Black community also established many schools for its children with the help of [Quakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quakers). Large-scale construction projects for new roads, [canals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canal), and railroads made Philadelphia the first major [industrial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution) city in the United States.

### 19th century

Further information: [Pennsylvania in the American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_in_the_American_Civil_War)



Opening day ceremonies at the [Centennial Exposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centennial_Exposition) at [Memorial Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorial_Hall_\(Philadelphia\)) in [Fairmount Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairmount_Park) in 1876, the first [world fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_fair) held in the U.S. on the centennial anniversary of the nation's founding

Throughout the 19th century, Philadelphia hosted a variety of industries and businesses; one of the largest was the [textile industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile_industry). Major corporations in the 19th and early 20th centuries included the [Baldwin Locomotive Works](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baldwin_Locomotive_Works), [William Cramp & Sons Shipbuilding Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Cramp_&_Sons_Shipbuilding_Company), and the [Pennsylvania Railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Railroad).[^cite_note-W\._W\._Norton_&_Company-1982-3]<sup>: 214, 218, 428–429 </sup> Established in 1870, the Philadelphia Conveyancers' Association was chartered by the state in 1871. Along with the U.S. Centennial in 1876, the city's industry was celebrated in the [Centennial Exposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centennial_Exposition), the first official [World's fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World's_fair) in the U.S.

Immigrants, mostly from Ireland and Germany, settled in Philadelphia and the surrounding districts. These immigrants were largely responsible for the [first general strike in North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1835_Philadelphia_general_strike) in 1835, in which workers in the city won the ten-hour workday. The city was a destination for thousands of Irish immigrants fleeing the [Great Famine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Famine_\(Ireland\)) in the 1840s; housing for them was developed south of [South Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Street_\(Philadelphia\)) and later occupied by succeeding immigrants. They established a network of [Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic) churches and schools and dominated the Catholic clergy for decades. Anti-Irish, anti-Catholic [nativist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativism_\(politics\)) [riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_nativist_riots) erupted in Philadelphia in 1844. The rise in population of the surrounding districts helped lead to the [Act of Consolidation of 1854](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_of_Consolidation,_1854), which extended the city limits from the 2 square miles (5.2 km<sup>2</sup>) of [Center City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_City,_Philadelphia) to the roughly 134 square miles (350 km<sup>2</sup>) of [Philadelphia County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_County).[^cite_note-57][^cite_note-Consolidation_Act_of_1854-58] In the latter half of the 19th century and leading into the 20th century, immigrants from Russia, Eastern Europe, and Italy, and African Americans from the [southern U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_U.S.) settled in the city.[^cite_note-59]

Philadelphia was represented by the [Washington Grays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Grays_\(Philadelphia\)) in the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War). The African-American population of Philadelphia increased from 31,699 to 219,559 between 1880 and 1930, largely stemming from the [Great Migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_\(African_American\)) from the [South](179553.md).[^cite_note-60][^cite_note-61]

### 20th century

By the 20th century, Philadelphia had an entrenched [Republican](32070.md) [political machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_machine) and a complacent population.[^cite_note-62] In 1910, [a general strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_general_strike_\(1910\)) shut down the entire city.[^cite_note-63]

In 1917, following outrage over the election-year murder of a Philadelphia police officer, the [City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_City_Council) shrank from two houses to just one.[^cite_note-64] In July 1919, Philadelphia was one of more than 36 industrial cities nationally to suffer a [race riot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_riots_in_Philadelphia_during_the_1919_Red_Summer) during [Red Summer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Summer) in post-[World War I](4764461.md) unrest as recent immigrants competed with Black residents for jobs. In the 1920s, the public flouting of [Prohibition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prohibition_in_the_United_States) laws, [organized crime](22625.md), mob violence, and corrupt police involvement in illegal activities led to the appointment of [Brig. Gen.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brig._Gen.) [Smedley Butler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smedley_Butler) of the [U.S. Marine Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Marine_Corps) as the city's director of public safety, but political pressure still prevented long-term success in fighting crime and corruption.[^cite_note-65]

In 1940, [non-Hispanic whites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Hispanic_whites) constituted 86.8% of the city's population.[^cite_note-66] In 1950, the population peaked at more than two million residents, then began to decline with the restructuring of industry that led to the loss of many middle-class union jobs. In addition, suburbanization enticed many affluent residents to depart the city for its outlying railroad commuting towns and newer housing. The resulting reduction in Philadelphia's tax base and the resources of local government caused the city to struggle through a long period of adjustment, and it approached bankruptcy by the late 1980s.[^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68]

In 1985, the [Philadelphia Police Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Police_Department), utilizing a [Pennsylvania State Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_State_Police) helicopter, [bombed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerial_bombing) the [Cobbs Creek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cobbs_Creek,_Philadelphia) neighborhood to execute arrest warrants on [MOVE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MOVE_\(Philadelphia_organization\)) members, a [Black liberation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_liberation) movement. [The incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985_MOVE_bombing) killed 11 people, destroyed 61 homes, and displaced 250 residents, marking one of the only times a US city intentionally bombed its own civilians.[^cite_note-69]

Revitalization and [gentrification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentrification) of neighborhoods began in the late 1970s and continues into the 21st century with much of the development occurring in the [Center City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_City,_Philadelphia) and [University City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_City,_Philadelphia,_Pennsylvania) neighborhoods. But this expanded a shortage of [affordable housing](3531662.md) in the city. After many manufacturers and businesses left Philadelphia or shut down, the city started attracting service businesses and began to market itself more aggressively as a tourist destination. Contemporary glass-and-granite [skyscrapers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_Philadelphia) were built in Center City beginning in the 1980s. Historic areas such as [Old City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_City,_Philadelphia) and [Society Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society_Hill) were renovated during the reformist mayoral era of the 1950s through the 1980s, making both areas among the most desirable Center City neighborhoods. Immigrants from around the world began to enter the U.S. through Philadelphia as their gateway, leading to a reversal of the city's population decline between 1950 and 2000, during which it lost about 25 percent of its residents.[^cite_note-70][^cite_note-71]

### 21st century

Philadelphia eventually began experiencing a growth in its population in 2007, which continued with incremental annual increases through the present.[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-United_States_Census_Bureau-73] A migration pattern has been established from New York City to Philadelphia by residents opting for a large city with relative proximity and a lower [cost of living](522934.md).[^cite_note-74][^cite_note-Katz2018-75]

## Geography

### Topography

Philadelphia's geographic center is about 40° 0′ 34″ north latitude and 75° 8′ 0″ west longitude. The [40th parallel north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/40th_parallel_north) passes through neighborhoods in [Northeast Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Philadelphia), [North Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Philadelphia), and [West Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Philadelphia) including [Fairmount Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairmount_Park). The city encompasses 142.71 square miles (369.62 km<sup>2</sup>), of which 134.18 square miles (347.52 km<sup>2</sup>) is land and 8.53 square miles (22.09 km<sup>2</sup>), or 6%, is water.[^cite_note-76] Natural bodies of water include the [Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_River) and [Schuylkill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schuylkill_River) rivers, lakes in [Franklin Delano Roosevelt Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_Delano_Roosevelt_Park), and [Cobbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cobbs_Creek), [Wissahickon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wissahickon_Creek), and [Pennypack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennypack_Creek) creeks. The largest artificial body of water is East Park Reservoir in Fairmount Park.

The lowest point is sea level and the highest point is in [Chestnut Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chestnut_Hill,_Philadelphia), about 446 feet (136 m) above sea level on Summit Street near the intersection of Germantown Avenue and [Bethlehem Pike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bethlehem_Pike) at: 40.07815 N, 75.20747 W.[^cite_note-77][^cite_note-78] Philadelphia is located on the [Atlantic Seaboard Fall Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Seaboard_Fall_Line) that separates the [Atlantic Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Plain) from the [Piedmont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piedmont_\(United_States\)).[^cite_note-79] The Schuylkill River's rapids at [East Falls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Falls) were inundated by completion of the dam at [Fairmount Water Works](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairmount_Water_Works).[^cite_note-80]

The city is the seat of [its own county](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_County,_Pennsylvania). The city is bordered by six adjacent counties: [Montgomery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montgomery_County,_Pennsylvania) to the northwest; [Bucks](91976.md) to the north and northeast; [Burlington County, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burlington_County,_New_Jersey) to the east; [Camden County, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camden_County,_New_Jersey) to the southeast; [Gloucester County, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gloucester_County,_New_Jersey) to the south; and [Delaware County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_County,_Pennsylvania) to the southwest.

### Cityscape

See also: [Architecture of Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Philadelphia) and [List of tallest buildings in Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_Philadelphia)

#### City planning

See also: [List of Philadelphia neighborhoods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Philadelphia_neighborhoods)



The skyline of Philadelphia seen from the northwest on [Spring Garden Street Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spring_Garden_Street_Bridge) over the [Schuylkill River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schuylkill_River) in April 2018 (annotated version)



Philadelphia's skyline at twilight from the southwest on [South Street Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Street_Bridge_\(Philadelphia,_Pennsylvania\)) with the [Schuylkill River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schuylkill_River) on the left in July 2016 (annotated version)

Philadelphia was created in the 17th century, following the plan by [William Penn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Penn)'s surveyor [Thomas Holme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Holme). [Center City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_City,_Philadelphia) is structured with long, straight streets running nearly due east–west and north–south, forming a grid pattern between the [Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_River) and [Schuylkill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schuylkill_River) rivers that is aligned with their courses. The original city plan was designed to allow for easy travel and to keep residences separated by open space that would help prevent the spread of fire.[^cite_note-Daly,_Molly-2011-81] In keeping with the idea of a "Greene Countrie Towne", and inspired by the many types of trees that grew in the region, Penn named many of the east–west streets for local trees.[^cite_note-82] Penn planned the creation of five public parks in the city which were renamed in 1824.[^cite_note-Daly,_Molly-2011-81] Centre Square was renamed [Penn Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penn_Square,_Philadelphia);[^cite_note-83] Northeast Square was renamed [Franklin Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_Square_\(Philadelphia\)); Southeast Square was renamed [Washington Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Square_\(Philadelphia\)); Southwest Square was renamed [Rittenhouse Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rittenhouse_Square); and Northwest Square was renamed [Logan Circle/Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logan_Circle_\(Philadelphia\)).[^cite_note-84] Center City had an estimated 183,240 residents as of 2015, making it the second-most populated downtown area in the United States after [Midtown Manhattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midtown_Manhattan) in New York City.[^cite_note-85]

Philadelphia's neighborhoods are divided into six large sections that surround Center City: [North Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Philadelphia), [Northeast Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Philadelphia), [South Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Philadelphia), [Southwest Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Philadelphia), [West Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Philadelphia), and [Northwest Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Philadelphia). The city's geographic boundaries have been largely unchanged since these neighborhoods were consolidated in 1854. However, each of these large areas contains numerous neighborhoods, some of whose boundaries derive from the boroughs, townships, and other communities that constituted [Pennsylvania County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_County,_Pennsylvania) before their inclusion within the city.[^cite_note-InsightGuides2005-14]<sup>: 58 </sup>

The [City Planning Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Planning_Commission_\(Philadelphia\)), tasked with guiding growth and development of the city, has divided the city into 18 planning districts as part of the Philadelphia2035 physical development plan.[^cite_note-86][^cite_note-87] Much of the city's 1980 zoning code was overhauled from 2007 to 2012 as part of a joint effort between former mayors [John F. Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Street) and [Michael Nutter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Nutter). The zoning changes were intended to rectify incorrect zoning maps to facilitate future community development, as the city forecasts an additional 100,000 residents and 40,000 jobs will be added by 2035.

The [Philadelphia Housing Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Housing_Authority) (PHA) is the largest landlord in Pennsylvania. Established in 1937, the PHA is the nation's fourth-largest housing authority, serving about 81,000 people with affordable housing, while employing 1,400 on a budget of \$371 million.[^cite_note-88] The [Philadelphia Parking Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Parking_Authority) is responsible for ensuring adequate parking for city residents, businesses, and visitors.[^cite_note-89]

#### Architecture

Main articles: [Architecture of Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Philadelphia) and [List of tallest buildings in Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_Philadelphia)



[Chestnut Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chestnut_Street_\(Philadelphia\)) in [Center City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_City,_Philadelphia) at night in February 2016



[Philadelphia City Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_City_Hall) at night in December 2012



Two of [Center City Philadelphia's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_City,_Philadelphia) most prominent high-rise buildings, [One Liberty Place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty_Place#One_Liberty_Place), built between 1985 and 1987 (in background), and [Philadelphia City Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_City_Hall), built between 1871 and 1901 (in foreground)

Philadelphia's architectural history dates back to [colonial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_history_of_the_United_States) times and includes a wide range of styles. The earliest structures were constructed with [logs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Log_house), but brick structures were common by 1700. During the 18th century, the [cityscape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cityscape) was dominated by [Georgian architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_architecture), including [Independence Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Hall) and [Christ Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_Church,_Philadelphia).

In the first decades of the 19th century, [Federal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_architecture) and [Greek Revival architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Revival_architecture) were the dominant styles produced by Philadelphia architects such as [Benjamin Latrobe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Latrobe), [William Strickland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Strickland_\(architect\)), [John Haviland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Haviland), [John Notman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Notman), [Thomas Walter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Ustick_Walter), and [Samuel Sloan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Sloan_\(architect\)).[^cite_note-W\._W\._Norton_&_Company-1982-3]<sup>: 11, 41, 174–175, 251–253 </sup> [Frank Furness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Furness) is considered Philadelphia's greatest architect of the second half of the 19th century. His contemporaries included [John McArthur Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_McArthur_Jr.), [Addison Hutton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Addison_Hutton), [Wilson Eyre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilson_Eyre), the [Wilson Brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilson_Brothers), and [Horace Trumbauer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horace_Trumbauer). In 1871, construction began on the [Second Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Empire_architecture_in_the_United_States_and_Canada)-style [Philadelphia City Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_City_Hall). The [Philadelphia Historical Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Historical_Commission) was created in 1955 to preserve the cultural and architectural history of the city. The commission maintains the [Philadelphia Register of Historic Places](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Register_of_Historic_Places), adding historic buildings, structures, sites, objects and districts as it sees fit.[^cite_note-90]

In 1932, Philadelphia became home to the first modern [International Style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Style) skyscraper in the United States, the [PSFS Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PSFS_Building), designed by [George Howe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Howe_\(architect\)) and [William Lescaze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Lescaze). The 548 ft (167 m) City Hall remained the tallest building in the city until 1987 when [One Liberty Place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty_Place#One_Liberty_Place) was completed. Numerous glass and granite skyscrapers were built in Center City beginning in the late 1980s. In 2007, the [Comcast Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comcast_Center) surpassed One Liberty Place to become the city's tallest building. The [Comcast Technology Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comcast_Technology_Center) was completed in 2018, reaching a height of 1,121 ft (342 m), as the [tallest building in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallest_building_in_the_United_States) outside of [Manhattan](45470.md) and Chicago.[^cite_note-91]

For much of Philadelphia's history, the typical home has been the [row house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Row_house). The row house was introduced to the United States via Philadelphia in the early 19th century and, for a time, row houses built elsewhere in the United States were known as "Philadelphia rows".[^cite_note-W\._W\._Norton_&_Company-1982-3]<sup>: 11, 41, 174–175, 251–253 </sup> A variety of row houses are found throughout the city, from Federal-style continuous blocks in [Old City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_City,_Philadelphia) and [Society Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society_Hill) to Victorian-style homes in [North Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Philadelphia) to twin row houses in [West Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Philadelphia). While newer homes have been built recently, much of the housing dates to the 18th, 19th, and early 20th centuries, which has created problems such as [urban decay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_decay) and vacant lots. Some neighborhoods, including [Northern Liberties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Liberties) and Society Hill, have been rehabilitated through [gentrification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentrification).[^cite_note-92][^cite_note-93]

- Elfreth's Alley, first developed in 1703, is the nation's oldest residential street.

  [Elfreth's Alley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elfreth's_Alley), first developed in 1703, is the nation's oldest residential street.

  [^cite_note-94]

- Carpenters' Hall, built between 1770 and 1774 in Georgian architecture style

  [Carpenters' Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpenters'_Hall), built between 1770 and 1774 in [Georgian architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_architecture) style

- The Second Bank of the United States, built between 1818 and 1824, exhibiting Greek Revival architecture

  The [Second Bank of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Bank_of_the_United_States), built between 1818 and 1824, exhibiting [Greek Revival architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Revival_architecture)

#### Parks

See also: [Drinking fountains in Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drinking_fountains_in_Philadelphia), [Fairmount Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairmount_Park), and [List of parks in Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_parks_in_Philadelphia)



Philadelphia's [Fairmount Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairmount_Park) on the [Schuylkill River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schuylkill_River), the city's largest and one of the world's largest public parks

As of 2014, the city's total park space, including municipal, state, and federal parks in the city, amounts to 11,211 acres (17.5 sq mi).[^cite_note-tpl.org-30] Philadelphia's largest park is [Fairmount Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairmount_Park), which includes the [Philadelphia Zoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Zoo) and encompasses 2,052 acres (3.2 sq mi) of the total parkland. Fairmount Park's adjacent [Wissahickon Valley Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wissahickon_Valley_Park) contains 2,042 acres (3.2 sq mi).[^cite_note-95] Fairmount Park, when combined with Wissahickon Valley Park, is one of the largest contiguous [urban park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_park) areas in the U.S.[^cite_note-tpl.org-30] The two parks, along with the [Colonial Revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_Revival), [Georgian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_architecture) and [Federal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_architecture)-style [mansions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_houses_in_Fairmount_Park) in them, have been listed as one entity on the [National Register of Historic Places](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Register_of_Historic_Places) since 1972.[^cite_note-96]

### Climate

| Philadelphia, Pennsylvania |
| --- |
| Climate chart ([explanation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:Climate_chart/How_to_read_a_climate_chart)) |
| J F M A M J J A S O N D        3.1     41 26       2.8     44 28       4     53 34       3.5     65 44       3.3     74 54       4     83 64       4.4     88 70       4.3     86 68       4.4     79 61       3.5     67 49       2.9     56 39       4     46 31   █ Average max. and min. temperatures in °F █ Precipitation totals in inches |
| Metric conversion  J F M A M J J A S O N D        80     5 −3       70     7 −3       101     12 1       88     18 7       85     24 12       103     28 18       111     31 21       109     30 20       112     26 16       88     20 10       74     13 4       101     8 0   █ Average max. and min. temperatures in °C █ Precipitation totals in mm |

Within the [Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification), Philadelphia falls under the northern periphery of the [humid subtropical climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_subtropical_climate) zone (Köppen *Cfa*),[^cite_note-97] while under the [Trewartha climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trewartha_climate_classification), the city is classified as a [maritime climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maritime_climate) (*Do*) bordered to the north by the [continental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_climate) (*Dc*) zone.[^cite_note-98] Summers are typically hot and muggy. Fall and spring are generally mild, and winter is moderately cold. The plant life [hardiness zones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardiness_zone#Selected_U.S._cities) are 7a and 7b, reflecting an average annual extreme minimum temperature between 0 and 10 °F (−18 and −12 °C).[^cite_note-99]

Snowfall is highly variable. Some winters have only light snow while others include major snowstorms. The normal seasonal snowfall averages 22.4 in (57 cm), with rare snowfalls in November or April, and rarely any sustained snow cover.[^cite_note-National_Oceanic_and_Atmospheric_Administration-2-100] Seasonal snowfall accumulation has ranged from trace amounts in 1972–73, to 78.7 inches (200 cm) in the winter of 2009–10.[^cite_note-National_Oceanic_and_Atmospheric_Administration-2-100][^cite_note-101] The city's [heaviest single-storm snowfall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_blizzard_of_1996#Philadelphia_and_Pennsylvania) was 30.7 in (78 cm), which occurred in January 1996.[^cite_note-102]

Precipitation is generally spread throughout the year, with eight to eleven wet days per month,[^cite_note-103] at an average annual rate of 44.1 inches (1,120 mm), but historically ranging from 29.31 in (744 mm) in 1922 to 64.33 in (1,634 mm) in 2011.[^cite_note-National_Oceanic_and_Atmospheric_Administration-2-100] The most rain recorded in one day occurred on July 28, 2013, when 8.02 in (204 mm) fell at [Philadelphia International Airport](262559.md).[^cite_note-National_Oceanic_and_Atmospheric_Administration-2-100] Philadelphia has a moderately sunny climate with an average of 2,498 [hours of sunshine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunshine_duration) annually. The percentage of sunshine ranges from 47% in December to 61% in June, July, and August.[^cite_note-National_Oceanic_and_Atmospheric_Administration-104]

The January daily average temperature is 33.7 °F (0.9 °C). The temperature frequently rises to 50 °F (10 °C) during thaws. July averages 78.7 °F (25.9 °C). Heat waves accompanied by high humidity and [heat indices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heat_indices) are frequent, with highs reaching or exceeding 90 °F (32 °C) on 30 days of the year. The average window for freezing temperatures is November 6 to April 2,[^cite_note-National_Oceanic_and_Atmospheric_Administration-2-100] allowing a growing season of 217 days. Early fall and late winter are generally dry, with February having the lowest average precipitation at 2.75 inches (70 mm). The dewpoint in the summer averages between 59.1 and 64.5 °F (15 and 18 °C).[^cite_note-National_Oceanic_and_Atmospheric_Administration-2-100]

The highest recorded temperature was 106 °F (41 °C) on August 7, 1918. Temperatures at or above 100 °F (38 °C) occur sporadically, with the most recent instances being three consecutive days from July 2–4, 2026, when the temperature reached 103 °F (39 °C), 102 °F (39 °C), and 101 °F (38 °C), respectively.[^cite_note-RecordTemps-105] The lowest officially recorded temperature was −11 °F (−24 °C) on February 9, 1934.[^cite_note-Philadelphia_Record_Highs_and_Lows-106] Temperatures at or below 0 °F (−18 °C) are rare, with the last such occurrence being [January 19, 1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_North_American_cold_wave).[^cite_note-National_Oceanic_and_Atmospheric_Administration-2-100] The record low maximum is 5 °F (−15 °C) on February 10, 1899, and December 30, 1880. The record high minimum is 83 °F (28 °C) on July 23, 2011, and July 24, 2010.[^cite_note-107] The record high dew point is 82 °F (28 °C) which occurred on July 15, 1995.[^cite_note-108]

#### Air quality

Philadelphia County received an [ozone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ozone) grade of F and a 24-hour [particle pollution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particulates) rating of D in the [American Lung Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Lung_Association)'s 2017 State of the Air report, which analyzed data from 2013 to 2015.[^cite_note-117][^cite_note-American_Lung_Association-118] The city was ranked 22nd for ozone, 20th for short-term particle pollution, and 11th for year-round particle pollution.[^cite_note-American_Lung_Association-2-119] According to the same report, the city experienced a significant reduction in high ozone days since 2001—from nearly 50 days per year to fewer than 10—along with fewer days of high particle pollution since 2000—from about 19 days per year to about 3—and an approximate 30% reduction in annual levels of particle pollution since 2000.[^cite_note-American_Lung_Association-118]

Five of the ten largest [combined statistical areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_statistical_area#List_of_combined_statistical_areas) (CSAs) were ranked higher for ozone: Los Angeles (1st), New York City (9th), [Houston](13774.md) (12th), [Dallas](53838.md) (13th), and [San Jose, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Jose,_California) (18th). Many smaller CSAs were also ranked higher for ozone, including [Sacramento](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacramento) (8th), [Las Vegas](47737.md) (10th), [Denver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denver) (11th), [El Paso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Paso) (16th), and [Salt Lake City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salt_Lake_City) (20th). Only two of those same ten CSAs, San Jose and Los Angeles, were ranked higher than Philadelphia for both year-round and short-term particle pollution.[^cite_note-American_Lung_Association-2-119]

As of the [2020 U.S. census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_U.S._census), there were 1,603,797 people residing in Philadelphia, representing a 1.2% increase from the 2019 census estimate.[^cite_note-United_States_Census_Bureau-73] The racial composition of the city was 39.3% Black alone (42.0% Black alone or in combination), 36.3% White alone (41.9% White alone or in combination), 8.7% Asian alone, 0.4% American Indian and Alaska Native alone, 8.7% some other race, and 6.9% multiracial. 14.9% of residents were Hispanic or Latino.[^cite_note-Explore_Census_Data-122]

34.8% had a bachelor's degree or higher. 23.9% spoke a language other than English at home, the most common of which was Spanish (10.8%). 15.0% of the population is foreign born, roughly half of whom are naturalized U.S. citizens. 3.7% of the population are veterans. The median household income was \$52,889 and 22.8% of the population lived in poverty. 49.5% of the population drove alone to work, while 23.2% used public transit, 8.2% carpooled, 7.9% walked, and 7.0% worked from home. The average commute is 31 minutes.[^cite_note-Explore_Census_Data-122]

After the [1950 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1950_census), when a record high of 2,071,605 was recorded, the city's population began a long decline. The population dropped to a low of 1,488,710 residents in 2006 before beginning to rise again. Between 2006 and 2017, Philadelphia added 92,153 residents. In 2017, the U.S. Census Bureau estimated that the racial composition of the city was 41.3% Black (non-Hispanic), 34.9% White (non-Hispanic), 14.1% Hispanic or Latino, 7.1% Asian, 0.4% Native American, 0.05% Pacific Islander, and 2.8% multiracial.[^cite_note-123]
