Philadelphia had historically been a bastion of the [Republican Party](32070.md) from the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War) until the mid-1930s.[^cite_note-282][^cite_note-283] In [1856](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1856_Republican_National_Convention), the first [Republican National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_National_Convention) was held at [Musical Fund Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musical_Fund_Hall) at 808 [Locust Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Locust_Street) in Philadelphia.[^cite_note-284]

Democratic registrations increased after the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression); however, the city was not carried by Democrat [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt) in his [landslide victory of 1932](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1932_United_States_presidential_election), as Pennsylvania was one of only six states won by Republican [Herbert Hoover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbert_Hoover). Voter turnout surged from 600,000 in 1932 to nearly 900,000 in [1936](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1936_United_States_presidential_election) and Roosevelt carried Philadelphia with over 60% of the vote. Philadelphia has voted Democratic in every presidential election since 1936. In [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_United_States_presidential_election), Democrat [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) drew 83% of the city's vote. Obama's win was even larger in [2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_United_States_presidential_election), capturing 85% of the vote. In [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election), Democrat [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton) won 82% of the vote.[^cite_note-285]

As a result of the previously declining population in the city and state,[^cite_note-286] Philadelphia has only three [congressional districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_district) of the 18 districts in Pennsylvania, based on the [2010 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_United_States_Census) apportionment:[^cite_note-287] [the 2nd district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania's_2nd_congressional_district), represented by [Brendan Boyle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brendan_Boyle); [the 3rd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania's_3rd_congressional_district), represented by [Dwight Evans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwight_Evans_\(politician\)); and [the 5th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania's_5th_congressional_district), represented by [Mary Gay Scanlon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Gay_Scanlon).[^cite_note-288] All three representatives are Democrats, though Republicans still have some support in the city, primarily in the [Northeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Philadelphia).[^cite_note-289] [Sam Katz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Katz_\(Philadelphia\)) ran competitive mayoral races as the Republican nominee in [1999](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_mayoral_election,_1999) and [2003](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_mayoral_election,_2003), losing to Democrat [John Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Street) both times.[^cite_note-290][^cite_note-291]

Pennsylvania's longest-serving [Senator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_senators_from_Pennsylvania), [Arlen Specter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arlen_Specter),[^cite_note-292] was an [alumnus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alumnus) of the [University of Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Pennsylvania) who opened his first law practice in Philadelphia.[^cite_note-Specter-293] Specter served as a Republican from 1981 and as a Democrat from 2009, losing that party's primary in [2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate_Democratic_primary_election_in_Pennsylvania,_2010) and leaving office in January 2011.[^cite_note-294] He was assistant counsel on the [Warren Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warren_Commission) in 1964 and the city's district attorney from 1966 to 1974.[^cite_note-Specter-293]

Philadelphia has hosted various [national conventions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_nominating_convention), including in [1848 (Whig)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1848_Whig_National_Convention), [1856 (Republican)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1856_Republican_National_Convention), [1872 (Republican)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1872_Republican_National_Convention), [1900 (Republican)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1900_Republican_National_Convention), [1936 (Democratic)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1936_Democratic_National_Convention), [1940 (Republican)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1940_Republican_National_Convention), [1948 (Republican)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Republican_National_Convention), [1948 (Progressive)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Progressive_National_Convention), [2000 (Republican)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Republican_National_Convention), and [2016 (Democratic)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Democratic_National_Convention).[^cite_note-295] Philadelphia has been home to one vice president, [George M. Dallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_M._Dallas),[^cite_note-296] and one general of the American Civil War, [George B. McClellan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_B._McClellan), who won his party's nomination for president but lost in the general election to [Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln) in 1864.[^cite_note-297] In May 2019, former U.S. Vice President [Joe Biden](145422.md) chose Philadelphia to be his [2020 U.S. presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_U.S._presidential_campaign) headquarters.[^cite_note-298]

### Environmental policy

"Green Cities, Clean Water" is an environmental policy initiative based in Philadelphia that has shown promising results in mitigating the effects of climate change.[^cite_note-Shade-2020-299] The researchers on the policy have stated that despite such promising plans of [green infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_infrastructure) building, "the city is forecasted to grow warmer, wetter, and more urbanized over the century, runoff and local temperatures will increase on average throughout the city".[^cite_note-Shade-2020-299] Even though landcover predictive models on the effects of the policy initiative have indicated that green infrastructure could be useful at decreasing the amount of runoff in the city over time, the city government would have to expand its current plans and "consider the cobenefit of [climate change adaptation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_adaptation) when planning new projects" in limiting the scope of city-wide temperature increase.[^cite_note-Shade-2020-299]

### Public safety

Further information: [Crime in Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_in_Philadelphia) and [Philadelphia crime family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_crime_family)

#### Police and law enforcement

Main article: [Philadelphia Police Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Police_Department)



The [Philadelphia Police Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Police_Department) administration building, known as the Roundhouse, in [Center City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_City,_Philadelphia) east of [Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown,_Philadelphia)



A Philadelphia police cruiser on [Benjamin Franklin Parkway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Franklin_Parkway)

In a 2015 report by Pew Charitable Trusts, the [police districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Police_Department#Districts) with the highest rates of violent crime were [Frankford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frankford,_Philadelphia) (15th district) and [Kensington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kensington,_Philadelphia) (24th district) in the [Near Northeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_Northeast_Philadelphia), and districts to the [North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Philadelphia) (22nd, 25th, and 35th districts), [West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Philadelphia) (19th district) and [Southwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Philadelphia) (12th district) of [Center City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_City,_Philadelphia). Each of those seven districts recorded more than a thousand violent crimes in 2014. The lowest rates of violent crime occurred in Center City, [South Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Philadelphia), the [Far Northeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_Northeast_Philadelphia), and [Roxborough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roxborough,_Philadelphia) districts, the latter of which includes [Manayunk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manayunk,_Philadelphia).[^cite_note-pewtrusts.org-2015-163]

Philadelphia had 500, 503 according to some sources, murders in 1990, a rate of 31.5 per 100,000. An average of about 400 murders occurred each year for most of the 1990s. The murder count dropped in 2002 to 288, then rose to 406 by 2006, before dropping slightly to 392 in 2007.[^cite_note-mikenutterllc.com-2021-300][^cite_note-301] A few years later, Philadelphia began to see a rapid decline in homicides and violent crime. In 2013, the city had 246 murders, which is a decrease of nearly 40% since 2006.[^cite_note-302]

In 2014, 248 homicides were committed. The homicide rate rose to 280 in 2015, then fell slightly to 277 in 2016, before rising again to 317 in 2017.[^cite_note-303] Homicides increased dramatically in the late 2010s/early 2020s, reaching 499 homicides in 2020[^cite_note-mikenutterllc.com-2021-300] and surpassing the 1990 "record" in 2021, with 501st murder on November 27 and 510 by the end of the month.[^cite_note-304] Phillie ended the year with 562 murders, an all-time record. It dropped in 2022 to 514, and significantly further again in 2023, to 410.[^cite_note-305] In 2006, Philadelphia's homicide rate of 27.7 per 100,000 people was the highest of the country's 10 most populous cities.[^cite_note-306] In 2012, Philadelphia had the fourth-highest homicide rate among the country's most populous cities. The rate dropped to 16 homicides per 100,000 residents by 2014 placing Philadelphia as the sixth-highest city in the country.[^cite_note-pewtrusts.org-2015-163]

The number of shootings in the city has declined significantly since the early years of the 21st century. Shooting incidents peaked at 1,857 in 2006 before declining nearly 44 percent to 1,047 shootings in 2014.[^cite_note-pewtrusts.org-2015-163] Major crimes have decreased gradually since a peak in 2006 when 85,498 major crimes were reported. The number of reported major crimes fell 11 percent in three years to 68,815 occurrences in 2014. [Violent crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violent_crime), which include homicide, rape, aggravated assault, and robbery, decreased 14 percent in three years to 15,771 occurrences in 2014.[^cite_note-pewtrusts.org-2015-163]

In 2014, Philadelphia enacted an ordinance decriminalizing the possession of less than 30 grams of [marijuana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marijuana) or eight grams of [hashish](14415.md); the ordinance gave police officers the discretion to treat possession of these amounts as a civil infraction punishable by a \$25 ticket, rather than a crime.[^cite_note-Nuri-2018-307][^cite_note-Goldstein-2017-308] At the time, Philadelphia was at the largest city in the nation to decriminalize the possession of marijuana.[^cite_note-Goldstein-2017-308] From 2013 to 2018, marijuana arrests in the city dropped by more than 85%.[^cite_note-Nuri-2018-307] The purchase or sale of marijuana remains a criminal offense in Philadelphia.[^cite_note-Goldstein-2017-308]

#### Firefighting

Main article: [Philadelphia Fire Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Fire_Department)

The Philadelphia Fire Department provides [fire protection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire_protection) and [emergency medical services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergency_medical_services) (EMS). The department's official mission is to protect public safety by quick and professional response to emergencies and the promotion of sound emergency prevention measures. This mandate encompasses all traditional [firefighting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Firefighting_in_the_United_States) functions, including fire suppression, with 60 engine companies and 30 ladder companies[^cite_note-309] as well as specialty and support units deployed throughout the city; specialized firefighting units for [Philadelphia International Airport](262559.md) and the [Port of Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Philadelphia); investigations conducted by the [fire marshal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire_marshal)'s office to determine the origins of fires and develop preventive strategies; [prevention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire_prevention) programs to educate the public; and support services including research and planning, management of the fire communications center within the city's [911](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9-1-1) system, and operation of the Philadelphia Fire Academy.

## Education

Main article: [Education in Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Philadelphia)

See also: [Free Library of Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Library_of_Philadelphia)

### Primary and secondary education

See also: [School District of Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_District_of_Philadelphia)



[William Penn Charter School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Penn_Charter_School), established in 1689, the nation's oldest [Quaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quaker) school

Education in Philadelphia is provided by many private and public institutions. The [School District of Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_District_of_Philadelphia) is the local school district, operating [public schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_school_\(government_funded\)), in all of the city.[^cite_note-310] The Philadelphia School District is the eighth-largest [school district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_district) in the nation[^cite_note-311] with 142,266 students in 218 traditional public schools and 86 [charter schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charter_schools) as of 2014.[^cite_note-312]

The city's K-12 enrollment in district–run schools dropped from 156,211 students in 2010 to 130,104 students in 2015. During the same time period, the enrollment in charter schools increased from 33,995 students in 2010 to 62,358 students in 2015.[^cite_note-pewtrusts.org-2015-163] This consistent drop in enrollment led the city to close 24 of its public schools in 2013.[^cite_note-313] During the 2014 school year, the city spent an average of \$12,570 per pupil, below the average among comparable urban school districts.[^cite_note-pewtrusts.org-2015-163]

Graduation rates among district-run schools, meanwhile, steadily increased in the ten years from 2005. In 2005, Philadelphia had a district graduation rate of 52%. This number increased to 65% in 2014, still below the national and state averages. Scores on the state's standardized test, the [Pennsylvania System of School Assessment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_System_of_School_Assessment) (PSSA) trended upward from 2005 to 2011 but subsequently decreased. In 2005, the district-run schools scored an average of 37.4% on math and 35.5% on reading. The city's schools reached their peak scores in 2011 with 59.0% on math and 52.3% on reading. In 2014, the scores dropped significantly to 45.2% on math and 42.0% on reading.[^cite_note-pewtrusts.org-2015-163]

Of the city's public high schools, including charter schools, only four performed above the national average on the [SAT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAT) (1497 out of 2400[^cite_note-314]) in 2014: [Masterman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julia_R._Masterman_School), [Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_High_School_\(Philadelphia\)), [Girard Academic Music Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Girard_Academic_Music_Program), and [MaST Community Charter School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MaST_Community_Charter_School). All other district-run schools were below average.[^cite_note-pewtrusts.org-2015-163]

### Higher education

See also: [List of colleges and universities in Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_colleges_and_universities_in_Philadelphia)



The [University of Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Pennsylvania) is an [Ivy League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivy_League) university founded in 1749 by [Benjamin Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Franklin).



[Temple University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_University) is the city's largest university by enrollment.

Medical and research facilities of the University of Pennsylvania School of Medicine and the [Children's Hospital of Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Children's_Hospital_of_Philadelphia). Philadelphia has the third-largest student concentration on the [East Coast](89126.md), with more than 120,000 college and university students enrolled within the city and nearly 300,000 in the metropolitan area.[^cite_note-315] More than 80 colleges, universities, trade, and specialty schools are in the Philadelphia region. One of the founding members of the [Association of American Universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_American_Universities) is in the city, the [University of Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Pennsylvania), an [Ivy League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivy_League) institution with claims to be the [first university in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_university_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-316]

The city's largest university by student enrollment is [Temple University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_University), followed by [Drexel University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drexel_University).[^cite_note-317] The city's nationally ranked research universities comprise the University of Pennsylvania, Temple University, Drexel University, and [Thomas Jefferson University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson_University). Philadelphia is also home to five schools of medicine: [Drexel University College of Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drexel_University_College_of_Medicine), [Perelman School of Medicine at the University of Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perelman_School_of_Medicine_at_the_University_of_Pennsylvania), [Philadelphia College of Osteopathic Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_College_of_Osteopathic_Medicine), [Temple University School of Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_University_School_of_Medicine), and Thomas Jefferson University's Sidney Kimmel Medical College. Hospitals, universities, and higher education research institutions in Philadelphia's four congressional districts received more than \$252 million in [National Institutes of Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Institutes_of_Health) grants in 2015.[^cite_note-318]

Other institutions of higher learning within the city's borders include:

- [Chestnut Hill College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chestnut_Hill_College)

- [Community College of Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_College_of_Philadelphia)

- [Curtis Institute of Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curtis_Institute_of_Music)

- [Holy Family University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Family_University)

- [La Salle University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Salle_University)

- [Moore College of Art and Design](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moore_College_of_Art_and_Design)

- [Peirce College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peirce_College)

- [Saint Joseph's University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Joseph's_University)

- [The Restaurant School at Walnut Hill College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Restaurant_School_at_Walnut_Hill_College)

## Media

See also: [Media in Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_in_Philadelphia)

### Newspapers

Philadelphia's two major [daily newspapers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newspaper) are *[The Philadelphia Inquirer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Philadelphia_Inquirer)*, first published in 1829—the third-oldest surviving daily newspaper in the country—and the *[Philadelphia Daily News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Daily_News)*, first published in 1925.[^cite_note-319] The *Daily News* has been published as an edition of the *Inquirer* since 2009.[^cite_note-320] Recent owners of the *Inquirer* and *Daily News* have included [Knight Ridder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knight_Ridder), [The McClatchy Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_McClatchy_Company), and [Philadelphia Media Holdings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Media_Holdings), with the latter organization declaring bankruptcy in 2010.[^cite_note-Launder,_William-2012-321] After two years of financial struggle, the newspapers were sold to [Interstate General Media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_General_Media) in 2012.[^cite_note-Launder,_William-2012-321] The two newspapers had a combined daily circulation of 306,831 and a Sunday circulation of 477,313 in 2013, the 18th-largest circulation in the country, and their collective website, Philly.com,[^cite_note-322] was ranked 13th in popularity among online U.S. newspapers by [Alexa Internet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexa_Internet) the same year.[^cite_note-323]

Smaller publications include the *[Philadelphia Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Tribune)* published five days each week for the [African-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American) community;[^cite_note-324] *[Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_\(magazine\))* magazine, a monthly regional magazine;[^cite_note-325] *[Philadelphia Style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Style)*, a glossy [luxury lifestyle](858665.md) publication;[^cite_note-326] *[Philadelphia Weekly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Weekly)*, a weekly alternative newspaper;[^cite_note-327] *[Philadelphia Gay News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Gay_News)*, a weekly newspaper for the [LGBT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT) community;[^cite_note-328] *[The Jewish Exponent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Jewish_Exponent)*, a weekly newspaper for the Jewish community;[^cite_note-329] *[Al Día](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_D%C3%ADa_\(Philadelphia\))*, a weekly newspaper for the [Latino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latinos) community;[^cite_note-330] and *[Philadelphia Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Metro)*, a free daily newspaper.[^cite_note-331]

Student-run newspapers include the [University of Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Pennsylvania)'s *[The Daily Pennsylvanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Daily_Pennsylvanian)*,[^cite_note-332] [Temple University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_University)'s *[The Temple News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Temple_News)*,[^cite_note-333] and [Drexel University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drexel_University)'s *[The Triangle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Triangle_\(newspaper\))*.[^cite_note-334]

### Radio

The first experimental radio license was issued in Philadelphia in August 1912 to [St. Joseph's College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Joseph's_University). The first [commercial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commercial_broadcasting) [AM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AM_broadcasting) radio stations began broadcasting in 1922: first [WIP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WTEL_\(AM\)), then owned by [Gimbels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gimbels) department store, followed by [WFIL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WFIL), then owned by [Strawbridge & Clothier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strawbridge_&_Clothier) department store, and [WOO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WOO_\(Philadelphia\)), a defunct station owned by [Wanamaker's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wanamaker's) department store, as well as [WCAU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WPHT) and [WDAS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WDAS_\(AM\)).[^cite_note-Bishop-2000-335]

As of 2018, the [FCC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FCC) lists 28 [FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FM_broadcasting) and 11 [AM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AM_broadcasting) stations for Philadelphia.[^cite_note-336][^cite_note-337] As of December 2017, the ten highest-rated stations in Philadelphia were [adult contemporary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adult_contemporary) [WBEB-FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WBEB-FM) (101.1), [sports talk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_talk) [WIP-FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WIP-FM) (94.1), [classic rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classic_rock) [WMGK-FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WMGK) (102.9), [urban adult contemporary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_adult_contemporary) [WDAS-FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WDAS-FM) (105.3), [classic hits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classic_hits) [WOGL-FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WOGL-FM) (98.1), [album-oriented rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Album-oriented_rock) [WMMR-FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WMMR-FM) (93.3), [country music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Country_music) [WXTU-FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WXTU) (92.5), [all-news](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-news) [KYW-AM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KYW-AM) (1060), [talk radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk_radio) [WHYY-FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WHYY-FM) (90.9), and urban adult contemporary [WRNB-FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WRNB) (100.3).[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339] Philadelphia is served by three non-commercial [public radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_radio) stations: WHYY-FM ([NPR](29697232.md)),[^cite_note-340] [WRTI-FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WRTI) (classical and jazz),[^cite_note-341] and [WXPN-FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WXPN-FM) (adult alternative music).[^cite_note-342]

### Television

In the 1930s, W3XE, an experimental station owned by [Philco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philco), was the Delaware Valley's first television station. In 1939, the station became the nation's first NBC affiliate, and later became KYW-TV. In 1952, WFIL, later renamed WPVI, premiered the television show *Bandstand*, which later became the nationally broadcast *[American Bandstand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Bandstand)* hosted by [Dick Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick_Clark).[^cite_note-343] In the 1960s, WCAU, WFIL-TV, and WHYY-TV were founded.[^cite_note-Bishop-2000-335]

Each of the nation's commercial networks has an [owned-and operated station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Owned-and-operated_television_stations_in_the_United_States) in Philadelphia: [WDPN-TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WDPN-TV) 2 ([MeTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MeTV) and its sister networks), [KYW-TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KYW-TV) 3 ([CBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBS)), [WPVI-TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WPVI-TV) 6 ([ABC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Broadcasting_Company)), [WCAU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WCAU) 10 ([NBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBC)), [WPHL-TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WPHL-TV) 17 ([The CW](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_CW) with [MyNetworkTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MyNetworkTV) on a second subchannel), [WFPA-CD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WFPA-CD) 28 ([UniMás](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UniM%C3%A1s)), [WTXF-TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WTXF-TV) 29 ([Fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_Broadcasting_Company)), [WPSG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WPSG) 57 (a CBS-owned independent station), [WPPX-TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WPPX-TV) 61 ([Ion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ion_Television)), [WWSI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WWSI) 62 ([Telemundo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telemundo)), and [WUVP-DT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WUVP-DT) 65 ([Univision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Univision)). The region is served also by [public broadcasting](178808.md) stations [WPPT-TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WPPT_\(TV\)) in Philadelphia, [WHYY-TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WHYY-TV) (licensed to [Wilmington, Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilmington,_Delaware) with facilities in Philadelphia and a repeater station in [Seaford, Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seaford,_Delaware)), [WLVT-TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WLVT-TV) in the [Lehigh Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lehigh_Valley), and [NJ PBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NJ_PBS) station WNJS, licensed across the Delaware to [Camden, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camden,_New_Jersey) .[^cite_note-344]

Philadelphia is also the headquarters city for [Comcast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comcast), the owner of NBC and Telemundo, along with WCAU and WWSI and the area's [regional sports network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_sports_network) [NBC Sports Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBC_Sports_Philadelphia), with those stations and NBCSP based out of the city's tallest building, the [Comcast Technology Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comcast_Technology_Center), and more Comcast operations in the [Comcast Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comcast_Center). Additionally, sister company [Comcast Spectacor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comcast_Spectacor) owns the [Xfinity Mobile Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xfinity_Mobile_Arena), [Stateside Live!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stateside_Live!) and the [Philadelphia Flyers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Flyers), and will own the [replacement arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_South_Philadelphia_Arena) currently scheduled for a 2031 completion.

As of 2023, the Philadelphia [media market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_market) is the [fifth-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_television_stations_in_North_America_by_media_market) in North America with over 7.8 million viewers[^cite_note-345]

## Infrastructure

### Transportation

Main article: [Transportation in Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_in_Philadelphia)

See also: [30th Street Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/30th_Street_Station), [List of SEPTA Metro stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_SEPTA_Metro_stations), and [Philadelphia International Airport](262559.md)



Philadelphia's [30th Street Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/30th_Street_Station) serves both [SEPTA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SEPTA) regional and [Amtrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak) national trains and is Amtrak's third-busiest train station in the nation.



The [Art Deco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Deco)-style [grand concourse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concourse) at 30th Street Station, one of the nation's busiest passenger train stations, built between 1927 and 1933



A [Market–Frankford Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market%E2%80%93Frankford_Line) train departing [52nd Street station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/52nd_Street_station_\(Market%E2%80%93Frankford_Line\))

Philadelphia is served by [SEPTA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SEPTA), which operates buses, trains, [rapid transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_transit) (as both subways and [elevated trains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elevated_trains)), [trolleys](30733.md), and [trackless trolleys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trackless_trolleys) (electric buses) throughout Philadelphia, the four Pennsylvania suburban counties of [Bucks](91976.md), [Chester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chester_County,_Pennsylvania), [Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_County,_Pennsylvania), and [Montgomery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montgomery_County,_Pennsylvania), in addition to service to [Mercer County, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercer_County,_New_Jersey) ([Trenton](57843.md)) and [New Castle County, Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Castle_County,_Delaware) ([Wilmington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilmington,_Delaware) and [Newark, Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newark,_Delaware)).[^cite_note-346] The city's subway system consists of two routes: the subway section of the [Market–Frankford Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market%E2%80%93Frankford_Line) running east–west under [Market Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_Street_\(Philadelphia\)) which opened in 1905 to the west and 1908 to the east of City Hall,[^cite_note-347] and the [Broad Street Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broad_Street_Line) running north–south beneath [Broad Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broad_Street_\(Philadelphia\)) which opened in stages from 1928 to 1938.[^cite_note-348]

Beginning in the 1980s, large sections of the [SEPTA Regional Rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SEPTA_Regional_Rail) service to the far suburbs of Philadelphia were discontinued due to a lack of funding for equipment and infrastructure maintenance.[^cite_note-349][^cite_note-350][^cite_note-351]

Philadelphia's [30th Street Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/30th_Street_Station) is a major railroad station on Amtrak's [Northeast Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Corridor) with 4.4 million passengers in 2017 making it the [third-busiest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_busiest_Amtrak_stations) station in the country after [New York City's Pennsylvania Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Station_\(New_York_City\)) and [Washington's Union Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Union_Station).[^cite_note-amtrak.com-2017-352] 30th Street Station offers access to Amtrak,[^cite_note-353] SEPTA,[^cite_note-354] and [NJ Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NJ_Transit) lines.[^cite_note-355] Over 12 million SEPTA and NJ Transit rail commuters use the station each year, and more than 100,000 people on an average weekday.[^cite_note-amtrak.com-2017-352]

The [PATCO Speedline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PATCO_Speedline) provides rapid transit service to [Camden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camden,_New_Jersey), [Collingswood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collingswood), [Westmont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westmont,_New_Jersey), [Haddonfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haddonfield), [Woodcrest (Cherry Hill)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherry_Hill,_New_Jersey), [Ashland (Voorhees)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voorhees,_New_Jersey), and [Lindenwold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lindenwold), New Jersey, from stations on [Locust Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Locust_Street) between 16th and 15th, 13th and 12th, and 10th and 9th streets, on Market Street at 8th Street, and at 7th and Race at [Franklin Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_Square_\(Philadelphia\)).[^cite_note-356]

#### Airports

See also: [Philadelphia International Airport](262559.md)



An aerial view of [Philadelphia International Airport](262559.md), the busiest airport in [Pennsylvania](23332.md) and 21st-busiest in the nation with over 13.6 million passengers in 2023

Philadelphia is served by two airports. [Philadelphia International Airport](262559.md) (PHL), the larger of the two, is 7 mi (11 km) south-southwest of [Center City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_City,_Philadelphia) on the boundary with [Delaware County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_County,_Pennsylvania), and provides scheduled domestic and international air service.[^cite_note-Philadelphia_International_Airport-357] As of 2023, Philadelphia International Airport is the [21st-busiest airport in the nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_busiest_airports_in_the_United_States) with over 13.6 million passengers. It is also among the world's busiest airports [measured by traffic movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World's_busiest_airports_by_traffic_movements), including takeoffs and landings.[^cite_note-358] Over 30 million passengers pass through the airport annually on 25 airlines, including all major domestic carriers. The airport has nearly 500 daily departures to over 120 destinations worldwide.[^cite_note-Philadelphia_International_Airport-357] [SEPTA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SEPTA)'s [Airport Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airport_Line_\(SEPTA\)) provides direct service between Center City railroad stations and Philadelphia International Airport.[^cite_note-359]

Philadelphia's second major airport, [Northeast Philadelphia Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Philadelphia_Airport) (PNE), is a [general aviation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_aviation) [relief airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relief_airport) in [Northeast Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Philadelphia), which provides general and corporate aviation.[^cite_note-360]

#### Roads



The 9,650-foot (2,940 m) long [Benjamin Franklin Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Franklin_Bridge) spans the [Delaware River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_River), connecting Philadelphia and [South Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Jersey).



The Schuylkill Expressway ([I-76](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_76_\(Ohio%E2%80%93New_Jersey\))) eastbound at [I-676](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I-676) and [US 30](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_30), also known as the Vine Street Expressway, in [Center City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_City,_Philadelphia)

[William Penn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Penn) planned Philadelphia with [numbered streets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Numbered_street#Philadelphia) traversing north and south, and streets named for trees, including [Chestnut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chestnut_Street_\(Philadelphia\)), [Walnut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walnut_Street_\(Philadelphia\)), and Mulberry (since renamed [Arch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arch_Street_\(Philadelphia\))) Streets, traversing east and west. The two main streets were named [Broad Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broad_Street_\(Philadelphia\)), the north–south [artery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arterial_road), later designated [Pennsylvania Route 611](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Route_611), and High Street, the east–west artery, which was later renamed [Market Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_Street_\(Philadelphia\)), converging at Centre Square which later became the site of [City Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_City_Hall).[^cite_note-361]

[Interstate 95](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_95_in_Pennsylvania), also known as the Delaware Expressway, traverses the southern and eastern edges of the city along the [Delaware River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_River) as the main north–south [controlled-access highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Controlled-access_highway), and connects Philadelphia with [Newark, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newark,_New_Jersey) and New York City to the north and [Baltimore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltimore) and Washington, D.C. to the south. The city is served by [Interstate 76](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_76_\(Ohio%E2%80%93New_Jersey\)), also known as the Schuylkill Expressway, which runs along the [Schuylkill River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schuylkill_River), intersecting the [Pennsylvania Turnpike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Turnpike) at [King of Prussia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Prussia,_Pennsylvania) and providing access to [Harrisburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harrisburg) and points west. [Interstate 676](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_676), also known as Vine Street Expressway, links I-95 and I-76 through Center City, running below street level between the eastbound and westbound lanes of [Vine Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vine_Street_\(Philadelphia\)). Entrance and exit ramps for the [Benjamin Franklin Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Franklin_Bridge) are near the eastern end of the expressway just west of the I-95 interchange.[^cite_note-OpenStreetMap-362]

[Roosevelt Boulevard and Expressway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roosevelt_Boulevard_\(Philadelphia\)), also known as [U.S. 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_1_in_Pennsylvania), connects [Northeast Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Philadelphia) with Center City via I-76 through [Fairmount Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairmount_Park). Woodhaven Road, also known as [Route 63](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Route_63), and Cottman Avenue, also known as [Route 73](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Route_73), serve the neighborhoods of Northeast Philadelphia, running between I-95 and the Roosevelt Boulevard. Fort Washington Expressway, also known as [Route 309](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Route_309), extends north from the city's northern border, serving [Montgomery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montgomery_County,_Pennsylvania) and [Bucks](91976.md) Counties. [U.S. Route 30](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_30_in_Pennsylvania), also known as [Lancaster Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancaster_Avenue), extends west from [West Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Philadelphia) to [Lancaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancaster,_Pennsylvania).[^cite_note-OpenStreetMap-362]

[Interstate 476](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_476), locally called the Blue Route,[^cite_note-363] traverses [Delaware County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_County,_Pennsylvania), bypassing the city to the west and serving the city's western suburbs, providing a direct route to [Allentown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allentown,_Pennsylvania), the [Poconos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poconos), and points north. [Interstate 276](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_276), the [Pennsylvania Turnpike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Turnpike)'s [Delaware River Extension](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_River_Extension), is a bypass and commuter route north of the city, which links to the [New Jersey Turnpike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_Turnpike) and New York City.[^cite_note-OpenStreetMap-362]

[Delaware River Port Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_River_Port_Authority) operates four bridges in the Philadelphia area, each of which crosses the Delaware River to [South Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Jersey): [Walt Whitman Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Whitman_Bridge) (I-76), the [Benjamin Franklin Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Franklin_Bridge) (I-676 and U.S. 30), [Betsy Ross Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Betsy_Ross_Bridge) ([New Jersey Route 90](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_Route_90)), and [Commodore Barry Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commodore_Barry_Bridge) ([U.S. 322](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._322)) in Delaware County, south of the city.[^cite_note-364] The [Burlington County Bridge Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burlington_County_Bridge_Commission) maintains two additional bridges that cross the Delaware River. [Tacony–Palmyra Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tacony%E2%80%93Palmyra_Bridge) connects [PA Route 73](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PA_Route_73) in the [Tacony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tacony,_Philadelphia,_Pennsylvania) section of Northeast Philadelphia with [New Jersey Route 73](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_Route_73) in [Palmyra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyra,_New_Jersey) in [Burlington County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burlington_County). [Burlington–Bristol Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burlington%E2%80%93Bristol_Bridge) connects [NJ Route 413](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_Route_413)/[U.S. Route 130](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_130) in [Burlington, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burlington,_New_Jersey) with [PA Route 413](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PA_Route_413)/[U.S. 13](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_13_in_Pennsylvania) in [Bristol Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bristol_Township,_Pennsylvania), north of Philadelphia.[^cite_note-365]

#### Bus service

Further information: [Philadelphia Greyhound Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Greyhound_Terminal)

The [Greyhound terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Greyhound_Terminal) is at 1001 Filbert Street (at 10th Street) in Center City, southeast of the [Pennsylvania Convention Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Convention_Center) and south of [Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown,_Philadelphia).[^cite_note-366] Several other bus operators provide service at the Greyhound terminal including [Fullington Trailways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fullington_Trailways),[^cite_note-367] [Martz Trailways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martz_Trailways),[^cite_note-368] [Peter Pan Bus Lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Pan_Bus_Lines),[^cite_note-369] and [NJ Transit buses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NJ_Transit_Bus_Operations).[^cite_note-370]

Other intercity bus services include [Megabus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megabus_\(North_America\)) with stops at [30th Street Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/30th_Street_Station) and the visitor center for [Independence Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Hall),[^cite_note-371] [BoltBus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BoltBus) (operated by Greyhound) at 30th Street Station,[^cite_note-372] [OurBus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OurBus) at various stops in the city.

#### Rail

Further information: [History of rail transport in Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_rail_transport_in_Philadelphia)



[Suburban Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suburban_Station) with [art deco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_deco) architecture at 16th Street and JFK Boulevard

Since the early days of [rail transportation in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transportation_in_the_United_States), Philadelphia has served as a hub for several major rail companies, particularly the [Pennsylvania Railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Railroad) and the [Reading Railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reading_Railroad). The Pennsylvania Railroad first operated [Broad Street Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broad_Street_Station_\(Philadelphia\)), then [30th Street Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/30th_Street_Station) and [Suburban Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suburban_Station), and the Reading Railroad operated [Reading Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reading_Terminal), now part of the [Pennsylvania Convention Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Convention_Center). The two companies also operated competing commuter rail systems in the area. The two systems now operate as a single system under the control of [SEPTA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SEPTA), the regional transit authority. Additionally, the [PATCO Speedline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PATCO_Speedline) subway system and [NJ Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NJ_Transit)'s [Atlantic City Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_City_Line) operate successor services to [South Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Jersey).[^cite_note-373]

In 1911, Philadelphia had nearly 4,000 electric [trolleys](30733.md) running on 86 lines.[^cite_note-374] In 2005, SEPTA reintroduced trolley service to the [Girard Avenue Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Girard_Avenue_Line), Route 15.[^cite_note-375] SEPTA operates six subway-surface trolleys that run on street-level tracks in [West Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Philadelphia) and subway tunnels in [Center City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_City,_Philadelphia), along with two surface trolleys in adjacent suburbs.[^cite_note-376]

Philadelphia is a regional hub of the [federally-owned](204768.md) [Amtrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak) system, with 30th Street Station being a primary stop on the Washington-Boston [Northeast Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Corridor) and the [Keystone Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keystone_Corridor) to [Harrisburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harrisburg) and [Pittsburgh](25101.md). 30th Street also serves as a major station for services via the Pennsylvania Railroad's former [Pennsylvania Main Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Main_Line_\(Pennsylvania_Railroad\)) to Chicago. As of 2018, 30th Street is Amtrak's third-busiest station in the country, after New York City and Washington.\[^cite_note-[www.amtrak.com-171](http://www.amtrak.com-171/)]

### Utilities

#### Water purity and availability

Further information: [Philadelphia Water Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Water_Department)



[Fairmount Water Works](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairmount_Water_Works), Philadelphia's second municipal waterworks, in December 1984

In 1815, Philadelphia began sourcing its water via the [Fairmount Water Works](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairmount_Water_Works) on the [Schuylkill River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schuylkill_River), the nation's first major urban water supply system. In 1909, the Water Works was decommissioned as the city transitioned to modern [sand filtration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sand_filter) methods.[^cite_note-377] [Philadelphia Water Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Water_Department) (PWD) provides [drinking water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drinking_water), [wastewater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wastewater) collection, and [stormwater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stormwater) services for Philadelphia, as well as surrounding counties. PWD draws about 57 percent of its drinking water from the [Delaware River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_River) and the balance from the Schuylkill River.[^cite_note-City_of_Philadelphia-378] The city has two filtration plants on the Schuylkill River and one on the Delaware River. The three plants can treat up to 546 million gallons of water per day, while the total storage capacity of the combined plant and distribution system exceeds one billion gallons. The wastewater system consists of three water pollution control plants, 21 pumping stations, and about 3,657 miles (5,885 km) of sewers.[^cite_note-City_of_Philadelphia-378]

#### Electricity

Further information: [PECO Energy Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PECO_Energy_Company)

[Exelon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exelon) subsidiary [PECO Energy Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PECO_Energy_Company), founded as the Brush Electric Light Company of Philadelphia in 1881 and renamed Philadelphia Electric Company (PECO) in 1902, provides electricity to about 1.6 million customers and more than 500,000 natural gas customers in the southeastern Pennsylvania area including the city of Philadelphia and most of its suburbs.[^cite_note-379] PECO is the largest electric and natural gas utility in the state with 472 power substations and nearly 23,000 miles (37,000 km) of electric transmission and distribution lines and 12,000 miles (19,000 km) of natural gas transmission, distribution, and service lines.[^cite_note-380]

#### Natural gas

Further information: [Philadelphia Gas Works](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Gas_Works)

[Philadelphia Gas Works](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Gas_Works) (PGW), overseen by the [Pennsylvania Public Utility Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Public_Utility_Commission), is the nation's largest municipally owned natural gas utility. PGW serves over 500,000 homes and businesses in the Philadelphia area.[^cite_note-381] Founded in 1836, the company came under city ownership in 1987 and has been providing the majority of gas distributed within city limits. In 2014, the [City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_City_Council) refused to conduct hearings on a \$1.86 billion sale of PGW, part of a two-year effort that was proposed by the mayor. The refusal led to the prospective buyer terminating its offer.[^cite_note-382][^cite_note-383]

#### Telecommunications

See also: [Area codes 215, 267, and 445](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Area_codes_215,_267,_and_445)

Southeastern Pennsylvania was assigned the [215](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Area_code_215) [area code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Area_code) in 1947 when the [North American Numbering Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Numbering_Plan) of the [Bell System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bell_System) went into effect. The geographic area covered by the code was split nearly in half in 1994 when [area code 610](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Area_code_610) was created, with the city and its northern suburbs retaining 215. [Overlay area code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overlay_area_code) 267 was added to the 215 service area in 1997, and 484 was added to the 610 area in 1999. A plan in 2001 to introduce a third overlay code to both service areas, [area code 445](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Area_code_445) to 215 and [area code 835](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Area_code_835) to 610, was delayed and later rescinded.[^cite_note-384] Area code 445 was implemented as an overlay for area codes 215 and 267 starting on February 3, 2018.[^cite_note-385]

## Sister cities



A [Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown,_Philadelphia) [paifang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paifang) at 10th and [Arch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arch_Street_\(Philadelphia\)) streets, a symbol of Philadelphia's [sister city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sister_city) relationship with [Tianjin](50821.md)

| City | Country | Date |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [Florence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florence)[^cite_note-386] | Italy | 1964 |
| [Tel Aviv](31453.md)[^cite_note-387] | Israel | 1966 |
| [Toruń](31258.md)[^cite_note-388] | Poland | 1976 |
| [Tianjin](50821.md)[^cite_note-389] | China | 1979 |
| [Incheon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incheon)[^cite_note-390] | South Korea | 1984 |
| [Douala](19997263.md)[^cite_note-391] | Cameroon | 1986 |
| [Nizhny Novgorod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nizhny_Novgorod)[^cite_note-392] | Russia | 1992 |
| [Frankfurt](10992.md)[^cite_note-393] | Germany | 2015 |

Philadelphia also has three partnership cities or regions:[^cite_note-cdiphila.org-394]

| City | Country | Date |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [Kobe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kobe)[^cite_note-395] | Japan | 1986 |
| [Abruzzo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abruzzo)[^cite_note-396] | Italy | 1997 |
| [Aix-en-Provence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aix-en-Provence)[^cite_note-397] | France | 1999 |

Philadelphia has eight official [sister cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sister_cities) as designated by the Citizen Diplomacy International (CDI) of Philadelphia:[^cite_note-cdiphila.org-394] Philadelphia has dedicated landmarks to its sister cities. The Sister Cities Park, a site of 0.5 acres (0.20 ha) at 18th and [Benjamin Franklin Parkway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Franklin_Parkway) in [Logan Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logan_Circle_\(Philadelphia\)), was dedicated in June 1976. The park was built to commemorate Philadelphia's first two sister city relationships, with [Tel Aviv](31453.md) and [Florence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florence). Toruń Triangle, honoring the sister city relationship with [Toruń](31258.md), Poland, was constructed in 1976, west of the [United Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Way) building at 18th Street and Benjamin Franklin Parkway. Sister Cities Park was redesigned and reopened in 2012, featuring an interactive fountain honoring Philadelphia's sister and partnership cities, a café and visitor center, children's play area, outdoor garden, boat pond, and a pavilion built to [environmentally friendly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmentally_friendly) standards.[^cite_note-398][^cite_note-399]

The Chinatown Gate, erected in 1984 and crafted by artisans from [Tianjin](50821.md), stands astride 10th Street, on the north side of its intersection with [Arch Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arch_Street), as a symbol of the sister city relationship. The CDI of Philadelphia has participated in the [U.S. Department of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Department_of_State)'s "Partners for Peace" project with [Mosul](200475.md), Iraq,[^cite_note-400] and in accepting visiting delegations from dozens of other countries.[^cite_note-401] In September 2025, [John Moolenaar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Moolenaar), chair of the [United States House Select Committee on Strategic Competition between the United States and the Chinese Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_Select_Committee_on_Strategic_Competition_between_the_United_States_and_the_Chinese_Communist_Party), requested that Philadelphia review its sister city agreement with Tianjin.[^cite_note-402]

[^cite_note-45]: &#x20;Description of the Lenape peoples (Delaware nations) historic territories inside the [divides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divides) of the frequently mountainous [landforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landforms) flanking the [Delaware River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_River)'s [drainage basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drainage_basin). These terrains encompass from South to North and then counter-clockwise: the shores from the east-shore mouth of the river and the sea coast to Western Long Island (all of both colonial [New Amsterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Amsterdam) and [New Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Sweden)), andportions of Western Connecticut up to the latitude of the Massachusetts corner of today's boundaries—making the eastern bounds of their influence, thence their region extended\:westerly past the region around [Albany, New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albany,_New_York) to the [Susquehanna River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susquehanna_River) side of the [Catskills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catskills), thensoutherly through the eastern [Poconos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poconos) outside the rival [Susquehannock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susquehannock) lands past [Eastern Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Pennsylvania) then southerly past the site of [Colonial Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Philadelphia) past the west bank mouth of the Delaware and extending south from that point along a stretch of sea coast in northern colonial [Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware). The [Susquehanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susquehanna_River)-[Delaware River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware_River) system's [watershed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drainage_basin) divided the frequently contested hunting grounds between the rival [Susquehannock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susquehannock) people and Lenape peoples, and the Catskills and Berkshires played a similar boundary role in the northern regions of their original colonial era range.

China is, far and away, the primary sending country, with 22,140 city residents who make up about 11 percent of the foreign-born population, according to a Pew Charitable Trusts analysis of Census data. More than a quarter of residents are immigrants or have a foreign-born parent, Pew reported, and 23 percent speak a foreign language at home.
